New Zealand freshwater snails provide evidence for the Red Queen hypothesis.
Sickle cell disease persists in human populations because heterozygotes (carriers with one copy of the gene) have resistance to malaria.
Parasites often evolve to be less lethal over time.
E. O. Wilson characterized parasites as “predators that eat prey in units of less than one.”
Parasitic castrators don't kill their hosts.
Hyperparasites are parasites of parasites.
Human knowledge of parasites dates back to ancient Egypt, Greece, and Rome.
Bram Stoker's Dracula can be viewed as a story about parasitism.
The word 'parasite' comes from Greek παράσιτος, meaning 'one who eats at the table of another.'
Some caterpillars self-medicate when parasitized by eating plants toxic to both themselves and their parasites
Aphids have symbiotic bacteria that make them almost immune to parasitoid wasps
The polydnavirus genes found in parasitoid wasps have become permanently incorporated into wasp DNA and are inherited
Parasitoid wasps can detect their prey by 'feeling' vibrations caused by hosts moving inside plant tissue
Female parasitoid wasps mark hosts they've already parasitized with chemical signals to avoid wasting eggs on occupied hosts.
Koinobiont parasitoid wasps let their host continue eating and growing
Spider wasps (Pompilidae) exclusively attack spiders, which are fast and dangerous prey
Host caterpillars may try to bite, wriggle, thrash, or drop off plants to escape parasitoid wasps
Parasitoid wasps are themselves vulnerable to hyperparasitoid wasps
Mistletoe spreads using sticky seeds that adhere to birds
Some mistletoe seeds can explosively launch themselves up to 15 meters to reach new host trees.
Beech drops (Epifagus virginiana) are among the most specialized parasitic plants
Even aquatic environments have parasitic plants
Parasitic plants can be keystone species that control invasive plants more than native species
Some parasitic plants can detect light reflecting from nearby plants and use chlorophyll levels to select hosts with more sugar content.
Toxoplasma tissue cysts can persist in the brain and other organs for the lifetime of the host, periodically rupturing and re-encysting.
Toxoplasma infection depletes tryptophan in the host's brain by activating enzymes that break down this amino acid
Infected mice lose their fear not just of cat urine, but of predators in general
Toxoplasma can be transmitted sexually in some animals including rats, sheep, and dogs — not just through the usual routes of ingestion.
The malaria parasite was discovered by Charles Louis Alphonse Laveran in 1880 in Algeria
Ronald Ross discovered that mosquitoes transmit malaria in 1897, but initially proved this in bird malaria
Malaria has shaped human evolution
A free malaria merozoite survives in the bloodstream for only about 60 seconds before it must enter a new red blood cell.
Mosquitoes are selective about which plants they feed on for sugar
Plasmodium undergoes meiosis (sexual reproduction) inside the mosquito, creating genetic diversity
Sporozoites spend 2-3 hours in the skin after a mosquito bite before most enter the bloodstream
Malaria was well known to ancient civilizations — Hippocrates described tertian and quartan fevers around 400 BCE.
The malaria incubation period is typically 11 days, but can occasionally last 2-8 years in rare cases
Tapeworm scolexes (heads) have both suckers and hooks for attachment — the pork tapeworm has 22-32 hooks arranged in two rows.
Tapeworms have no digestive system
Adult pork tapeworms can survive 2-5 years in the human intestine, according to evidence from a British military outbreak in the 1930s.
The 1940s treatment for tapeworms involved inserting oleoresin of aspidium directly into the duodenum via a tube — primitive but effective.
A vaccine to prevent cysticercosis in pigs has been developed — breaking the parasite's life cycle in pigs could protect humans.
In 1922, scientist Shimesu Koino deliberately swallowed 2,000 Ascaris eggs to prove the parasite's life cycle
Ascaris eggs have a lipid layer that makes them resistant to acids, alkalis, and many chemicals
Some scientists have proposed that Ascaris lumbricoides (human roundworm) and Ascaris suum (pig roundworm) are actually the same species.
Ascariasis is the most common cause of Löffler's syndrome (pulmonary infiltrates with eosinophilia) worldwide
Antonie van Leeuwenhoek first observed the parasite Giardia lamblia with his microscope in 1681
Lamprey fish are micropredators that attach to larger fish and feed on their blood
Vampire bats are true micropredators
Scale insects are plant parasites so specialized they cannot move as adults
Coinfection by multiple parasites at once is common in nature — hosts often carry several different parasite species simultaneously.
The largest Rafflesia flower ever recorded measured 111 cm (44 inches) in diameter and was found in West Sumatra in January 2020.
Even the smallest Rafflesia species (R. baletei) produces flowers 12 cm across — enormous compared to its host vine.
Rafflesia evolved from plants with tiny flowers in the Euphorbiaceae family.
Tree shrews and other forest mammals eat Rafflesia berries and likely help disperse their seeds.
All Rafflesia species are found only in Southeast Asia — the genus has very limited geographic distribution despite having over 40 species.
Fish with two or more tongue-eating lice are usually underweight because eating becomes difficult
When a host fish dies, the tongue-eating louse detaches and clings to the outside of the fish's head or body
Cymothoa exigua parasitizes at least 8 fish species across 4 families, living in waters from 2 to 60 meters deep.
When a tongue-eating louse is separated from its host and handled, it may bite
Sacculina parasites can reach infection rates of 50% in some crab populations
Sacculina's life span matches that of its crab host, typically 1-2 years; the parasite is completely dependent on its host's survival.
Sacculina larvae look like typical barnacle larvae
Infected crabs stop molting, lose the ability to regenerate lost claws, and suffer reduced nutrition
Over 100 species of Sacculina parasitic barnacles have been identified, each specialized for different crab hosts.
The emerald cockroach wasp's second sting directly targets the subesophageal ganglion and supraesophageal ganglion in the cockroach brain
The jewel wasp's venom blocks octopamine receptors
Female jewel wasps only need to mate once to parasitize several dozen cockroaches over their several-month lifespan.
Scientists can recreate the zombie state in cockroaches by injecting procaine into the same brain region the wasp targets
Hairworms don't actively make grasshoppers seek water from far away
Adult hairworms are free-living aquatic organisms that reproduce in water — the parasitic stage is only the larval period inside the insect.
Hairworm parasites produce proteins from the Wnt signaling family that act on the host's nervous system development
Euhaplorchis sterilizes the horn snails it infects
Researchers estimate that snail populations in California marshes would be nearly double their current size without Euhaplorchis parasitism.
In negative frequency-dependent selection, rare host genotypes have an advantage because parasites haven't evolved to attack them yet
Geographic mosaic theory predicts that host-parasite coevolution varies across space
Caenorhabditis elegans nematodes coevolving with Bacillus thuringiensis bacteria show increased genetic diversity
Better understanding of host-parasite coevolution could lead to novel treatments for diseases like malaria, AIDS, and influenza
Lianas (climbing woody vines) in tropical forests can be considered generalist macro-parasites of trees
Guinea worm disease will be the first parasitic disease eradicated through behavioral change alone
More than 100 million Guinea worm cases have been prevented since the eradication program began
Guinea worm larvae can survive in copepods (water fleas) and develop to an infectious stage within just 14 days.
The scientific name 'Dracunculus medinensis' means 'little dragon from Medina'
Guinea worm infections peak during drought or dry season in humid climates
Snails infected by Leucochloridium can survive for at least a year and continue seeing through their infected tentacles
Snails can be infected by multiple Leucochloridium species simultaneously
Leucochloridium broodsacs can burst spontaneously from snail tentacles and continue pulsating for up to an hour
Leucochloridium uses a truncated life cycle
Leucochloridium exemplifies 'aggressive mimicry'
Infections with Leucochloridium often reduce or destroy the snail's reproductive organs
The broodsac colors of different Leucochloridium species are distinctive
Russian researchers found that Leucochloridium sporocysts produce infectious metacercariae starting in spring but die by late summer
Parasitoid wasps can undergo polyembryony
The smallest insects in the world are parasitoid wasps
Over 70 species of Anopheles mosquitoes can transmit Plasmodium falciparum malaria, out of approximately 460 Anopheles species total.
Malaria sporozoites use a unique gliding motility powered by actin and myosin motors beneath their membrane
A typical infectious mosquito bite delivers only 20-200 malaria sporozoites — yet this is often enough to establish infection.
The Plasmodium falciparum genome was fully sequenced in 2002
Cerebral malaria occurs when parasitized red blood cells stick to blood vessels in the brain
Inside mosquito midguts, male malaria gametocytes undergo 'exflagellation'
A single Plasmodium oocyst on the mosquito gut wall can produce over 3,000 sporozoites ready to infect new human hosts.
Only 15-20% of injected malaria sporozoites enter the lymphatic system, where they're mostly destroyed by CD8+ T cells
Mast cells in human skin detect malaria sporozoites and release warning signals that activate neutrophils and macrophages
Tapeworm proglottids (body segments) grow from the neck region
Tapeworms are acoelomates — they have no body cavity between their gut and body wall, and no circulatory or respiratory systems.
Tapeworm oncospheres (embryos) have six hooks that help them burrow through the intestinal wall to reach muscles or the brain.
Cysticercosis from pork tapeworm can cause epilepsy, dementia, and increased intracranial pressure
Tapeworm cysts can remain viable in pig muscle for several years, waiting to be consumed by a human host.
Pork tapeworm infections are most common where humans live in close contact with free-roaming pigs and eat undercooked pork.
Dirty hands are the primary cause of human cysticercosis — especially common among food handlers who carry and spread tapeworm eggs.
Ocular cysticercosis (eye cysts) is more common in children than adults, and can cause blindness if untreated.
The tapeworm uterus has a characteristic branching pattern with 7-13 branches per side — used to identify species under microscopy.
Ascaris worms can migrate to unusual locations if disturbed — sometimes emerging from the nose, mouth, or even through the skin.
Heavy Ascaris infections can cause intestinal obstruction, with tangles of worms blocking the gut — especially dangerous in children.
Ascariasis remains endemic in the southeastern United States due to warm, humid conditions favorable for egg development in soil.
Ascaris eggs require temperatures of 15-35°C and moist conditions for 18 days to several weeks to become infectious.
Ascaris anatomically exhibits sexual dimorphism — males have a curved tail end while females have a straight posterior.
Unfertilized Ascaris eggs are longer and thinner than fertilized ones and are never infectious
Hot composting at over 50°C for 24 hours can kill Ascaris eggs — one of the few reliable methods to destroy them.
Control of roundworm infections relies on treatment, improved sanitation, and health education — treatment typically takes about three days.
Hookworms for helminthic therapy are administered through the skin
Heavy hookworm infections can cause anemia because the worms feed on blood from the intestinal mucosa.
The prevalence of autoimmune diseases like multiple sclerosis is lower in countries where helminth infections are common
The zombie-ant fungus produces sphingosine and guanidinobutyric acid
Ophiocordyceps secretes hypoxanthine, which has damaging effects on neural tissues — a possible mechanism for controlling ant motor neurons.
The death grip of zombie ants is caused by muscle atrophy
Zombie-ant fungal cells form interconnected networks through anastomosing tubules
The zombie-ant fungus secretes different metabolites depending on which host tissue it encounters
When zombie ants are experimentally moved to different locations, the fungus fails to produce normal reproductive structures
Ant species not normally infected in nature can be killed by Ophiocordyceps but show no manipulated behavior
In temperate forests, zombie ants attach to twigs rather than leaves
The death-grip bite leaves distinctive dumbbell-shaped marks on leaves
Red naphthoquinone pigments from the zombie-ant fungus are stable, non-cytotoxic, and change color with pH
Polyketides from Ophiocordyceps have shown antitumor, antioxidant, and antiaging activities in preliminary studies.
What appear to be Rafflesia's petals are actually modified sepals (petaloid tepals)
Most Rafflesia species are dioecious (separate male and female plants), but R. baletei and R. verrucosa have hermaphroditic flowers.
Rafflesia shows remarkable homoplasy
Rafflesia forms four distinct geographic clades: Philippines, Peninsular Malaysia, Borneo, and Sumatra+Java
The Indonesian name 'padma raksasa' for Rafflesia arnoldii means 'giant lotus'
Coevolution experiments show that hosts evolving with parasites have higher recombination rates
Coevolution with bacteriophages can increase bacterial mutation rates
Pseudomonas bacteria coevolving with their phages tend to become generalists
Host-parasite coevolution may promote allopatric diversity
Parasites adapt to the most common host genotype in a population, creating selection pressure that favors rare genotypes
Directional selection in host-parasite evolution can produce evolutionary 'arms races' — repeated fixation of new attack and defense traits.
Unicellular hosts like bacteria evolve against parasites primarily through selective sweeps
Multihost parasites face evolutionary trade-offs — adaptations that improve infection in one host may reduce success in others.
Cats purr at a frequency between 25 and 150 Hertz
Cats cannot taste sweet things due to a defect in the gene that codes for part of the mammalian sweet taste receptor
About half of all cats don't respond to catnip at all
T. gondii cysts in the brain are not dormant as previously thought
Elephants have an incredible 40,000 to 50,000 muscles in their trunks alone

Fossil evidence shows penguin ancestors survived the mass extinction that killed the dinosaurs
Penguin coloring is camouflage
Flamingos are born with grey and white feathers and straight beaks
Flamingos feed with their heads upside down
Adult flamingos are 4-5 feet tall but weigh only 4-8 pounds - an astonishing body density that enables flight.
Flamingo parents produce 'crop milk' in their throats
A banana is a berry, but a strawberry is not
Continental tectonic plates drift at the same speed fingernails grow - up to 20 millimeters per year.
Reindeer eyes change color through the seasons - they're gold during summer and blue in winter.
Goats have rectangular pupils
Army ants can break from their scent trails and march in circles
The manchineel tree is called the 'tree of death'
China owns all giant pandas in the world and rents them to zoos for about $1 million per year.
Bones found at Seymour Island indicate that 37-40 million years ago, penguins stood 6 feet tall and weighed 250 pounds
Chihuahuas have the biggest brains relative to their body size in the dog world, making them very easy to train.
Kea parrots are the first known non-mammal species to communicate with infectious laughter - they warble together when in a good mood.
An avocado never ripens on the tree
The WAFFLE House Index is informally used by FEMA to gauge storm severity
Scientists recovered a record-breaking 1,268-meter-long core sample from the Lost City Hydrothermal Field in 2024
The Lost City ecosystem doesn't depend on sunlight or magma heat
Cats have a gene called Taqpep that determines whether they have stripes, spots, or blotched patterns
Male cats have barbed penises with 120+ backward-pointing spines
In 2015, scientists captured the first video of an elephant yawning - until then, it was only speculation that elephants yawned.
At Zambia's Mfuwe Lodge, families of elephants walk through the hotel lobby every October-December to eat wild mango from a courtyard tree.
Berlin zoos feed unsold Christmas trees to their elephants each January - the trees are certified pesticide-free.
Gentoo penguins are the fastest penguin species, reaching swimming speeds up to 22 mph.
For some penguin species, pudgy males with enough fat to survive weeks without eating are most desirable mates
Scientists have catalogued 402 bright streaks (lineae) on Mercury's surface that tend to cluster on sun-facing slopes of craters
Great white sharks undergo a major dental transformation at around 3 meters in body length
Great white shark teeth are continuously replaced like a conveyor belt, roughly every few weeks
The first two teeth on either side of a great white's jaw are significantly thicker at the base
Each dolphin has its own unique signature whistle that scientists believe they use for life
A dolphin's skin can be completely sloughed off and replaced with new skin cells as often as every two hours
Dolphins have multiple stomach chambers
Dolphin calves are born tail first rather than head first, which prevents drowning during birth.
The orca (killer whale) is actually a dolphin species; at 30 feet long, it's 10 times longer than the smallest dolphins.
Dolphin pods can merge to form 'superpods' of more than 1,000 dolphins. These typically form temporarily in areas with plentiful food.

Great white sharks have different tooth shapes across their jaw
Sharks are older than trees
Polar bears in some populations are getting fatter, surprising scientists who expected climate change to reduce their food sources.
Truffle-hunting dogs are advancing scientific understanding of fungi by locating previously unknown species underground.

Unlike virtually all other birds, the New Caledonian crow has a bill that doesn't curve downward
80% of American crow nests have 'helpers'
Goats' horizontal rectangular pupils give them a sharp panoramic view that lets them see nearly all around themselves
Goats' eyes rotate to keep their horizontal pupils aligned with the ground whether their heads are upright or face-down while grazing.
Pupil shape reveals an animal's role in the food chain
Beyond round, horizontal, and vertical pupils, some animals have w-shaped, crescent-shaped, or even heart-shaped pupils
The world's first global carbon tax was about to be introduced.
Global warming is forcing Earth's systems toward 'doom loop' tipping points.
All mammals studied show signs of REM sleep, suggesting that mammals may dream like humans.
Giraffes sleep only about 4.6 hours per day, and their sleep cycles are just 35 minutes or shorter.
Elephants may sleep as little as 2 hours per day and can travel for nearly two days without sleeping.
Little brown bats sleep about 20 hours per day, some of which is spent in torpor.
Dogs spend over a third of their day asleep, with another 21% in drowsy relaxation.
Horses can sleep standing up during NREM sleep but must lie down for REM sleep due to muscle atonia.
Dolphins, eared seals, and manatees sleep unihemispherically—one brain hemisphere sleeps while the other stays awake.
Walruses can sleep for 19.4-20.5 hours per day on land, but can also go up to 84 hours without sleep while swimming.
Walruses can hook their tusks onto ice floes and sleep while hanging in the water.
Birds can sleep unihemispherically, with one eye closed corresponding to the sleeping hemisphere of the brain.
Alpine swifts have been documented flying continuously for 200 days, likely sleeping unihemispherically while gliding.
Migratory white-crowned sparrows get only a third of their normal sleep during migration, compensating with micro-naps.
Bats and perching birds have locking tendons in their feet that allow them to sleep while hanging or perched without falling.
The Madagascan fat-tailed dwarf lemur is the only primate known to truly hibernate.
Reptiles like the Australian dragon (Pogona vitticeps) show sleep states similar to REM and NREM sleep.
Snakes sleep with both eyes open because they have no eyelids, and can sleep for days while digesting food.
Crocodiles can engage in unihemispheric sleep, keeping one eye open to watch for threats and prey.
Parrotfish secrete a cocoon of mucus around themselves for protection while they sleep.
Spanish hogfish and blue-headed wrasse can be lifted by hand to the surface without waking when in deep sleep.
Some sharks that breathe through ram ventilation (keeping their mouths open while swimming) may sleep one hemisphere at a time.
Fruit flies (Drosophila) sleep, and sleep deprivation in flies leads to cognitive disabilities similar to those in mammals.
Jumping spiders show REM-like sleep with regularly occurring bouts of retinal movements.
Octopuses have a two-staged sleep similar to REM and NREM, with color changes during 'active sleep.'
Sleep-like states have been observed in jellyfish (Cassiopea) and hydra, organisms without brains or central nervous systems.
Even sponges show evidence of sleep-like states, suggesting sleep may have evolved hundreds of millions of years ago.
In the nematode C. elegans, a lethargus sleep-like phase occurs before each molt, suggesting sleep is connected to development.
Salamanders can enter brumation (reptile hibernation) for up to 100 days at a time.
Green-striped burrowing frogs can enter estivation during dry periods, burrowing deep underground for months without moving or eating.
The only eyes that move during bird unihemispheric sleep are those connected to the awake hemisphere.
Nocturnal animals have higher body temperatures and serotonin levels at night—the inverse of diurnal animals.
Small species with higher metabolic rates may have less efficient sleep patterns than larger animals.
Birds exposed to predator risk have less deep sleep than birds in protected environments.
Cockroaches show a rest period characterized by folded antennae and decreased sensitivity to stimuli.
Crayfish show EEG changes during sleep that differ markedly from their awake patterns.
Cave-dwelling blind fish may not sleep due to the constant darkness eliminating circadian cues.
Zebrafish become motionless at night and float in place during their sleep period.
Sleep patterns in fish can disappear during migration, spawning, and parental care periods.
Marmosets can achieve monophasic sleep because they sleep in trees surrounded by protective family members.
Scientists can tell elephants are sleeping when their trunks stop moving.
Nurse sharks, which breathe by pumping water through their cheeks, can rest motionless on the seafloor.
Lizards may choose sleep perches with heads oriented toward predator approach paths for faster response.
REM-like sleep has recently been found in fish, suggesting this sleep type evolved earlier than previously thought.
The Kola Superdeep Borehole in Russia reaches 12,262 meters, the deepest artificial point on Earth, where temperatures reach 180°C.
The Challenger Deep in the Mariana Trench reaches 11,034 meters below sea level - the deepest known point in Earth's oceans.
Mauna Kea in Hawaii rises 10,203 meters from its oceanic base - taller than Mount Everest when measured from base to peak.
