Due to the extreme heat of its desert environment, the slender-horned gazelle is crepuscular, feeding mostly at night and early morning.
Sperm whales possess the most asymmetrical skull of any mammal.
Sperm whales have the largest brain of any living animal, weighing up to 4.2 kg (9.2 lbs.).
The longest recorded dive for a sperm whale was in excess of 2 hours.
Hyenas live in territorial social groups called clans. Clans are dominated by females and can reach up to 70 members.
The spotted hyena is the largest species of hyena. The other species are the brown hyena and the striped hyena.
Female hyenas have reproductive organs similar in appearance to males', making accurate sexing difficult.
The hyena is more closely related to the mongoose and cat than the dog.
Sugar gliders live in large colonies of 20 to 40 individuals with two alpha males fathering the majority of offspring.
Young gliders usually leave around 10 months to start their own colonies.
Tapirs, depending on the species, are found at altitudes ranging from sea level to 4,500 m (14,763 ft).
Thomson's gazelles (called Tommies) live in buck or doe herds of up to 100 individuals, or mixed groups of up to 700.
Tommies are easily identified by their tail motion: it swings side to side like a windshield wiper.
Males mark territory boundaries by whistling.
Kob breed year-round in East Africa, with an 8-month gestation and generally one offspring.
In order to evade a predator, kobs will leap into the air or seek refuge in water or reed beds.
Vaquitas are the smallest porpoise and among the smallest cetaceans.
Young opossums must spend the first two months of their lives in their mother's pouch.
Opossums have remained virtually unchanged from prehistoric times to the present; some scientists call them living fossils.
Opossums have fifty razor-sharp teeth, the highest number of teeth found in any land mammal.
Warthogs travel in groups called sounders consisting of 1 or 2 sows and young offspring. Males usually travel alone.
Boars have more prominent warts than sows. They are primarily used to protect the face during fights.
Although they look fierce, warthogs would rather run than fight, but they can be fierce opponents if forced.
Weddell seals belong to the scientific order Pinnipedia, which includes seals, sea lions, and walruses.
Weddell seals are named for Captain James Weddell, an explorer in the 1820s whose book described and illustrated Weddell seals.
Weddell seals have the southernmost distribution of any pinniped.
Tarsiers are capable of turning their heads nearly 180° in each direction, allowing them to rotate their heads almost 360°.
Tarsiers get their name from their long tarsal (ankle) bones, which help propel them while leaping.
The flat, fleshy pads at the end of tarsiers’ fingers and toes allow them to climb smooth vertical surfaces.
Tarsiers live in pairs.
Gibbons are among the 6% of primate species that are monogamous.
Contrary to many ape species, adult female gibbons are dominant in their family social structures.
Florida made the alligator its official state reptile in 1987; it is also the University of Florida at Gainesville's mascot.
There are two true species of alligators: American and Chinese. The Chinese gator is smaller than its American cousin.
American crocodiles hunt by remaining completely motionless in the water. When prey is close, they attack.
Crocodiles swallow stones to aid in digestion and to control buoyancy in the water.
Black rat snakes are among North America's longest snakes, occasionally reaching lengths of 8 feet.
When threatened, rat snakes will "rattle" their tail, fooling other animals into believing they are venomous.
Like pythons and boas, rat snakes are constrictors, which suffocate their prey.
Black rat snakes are excellent climbers and hunt for birds and eggs in trees.
Like all snakes, boas are excellent swimmers, but they usually avoid going into the water as much as possible.
Of the Python molurus subspecies, the Burmese python is the one most commonly found in managed situations.
The black caiman, Melanosuchus niger, is the largest member of the family Alligatoridae.
Cuvier's dwarf caiman, Paleosuchus palpebrosus, is the smallest member of the order Crocodylia.
The common caiman, Caiman crocodilus, is the most numerous of all crocodilians.
The common caiman, C. crocodilus, is the most hunted of all crocodilians, accounting for 60–80% of the skin trade.
The skin of Cuvier's dwarf caiman, P. palpebrosus, is heavily ossified.
Rattlesnakes have a special feeding system based on venom, injected into prey through teeth called fangs.
The rattlesnake uses its rattle to warn other animals of its presence, so it doesn't have to waste venom on defense.
Nesting behavior depends on the range of a particular carpet python.
The Cuban iguana is a subspecies of West Indian rock iguana.
Iguanas store large amounts of fat in their lower jaw and neck area in order to survive periods of famine.
The iguana's dewlap helps to regulate body temperature and is used in threat and courtship displays.
Iguanas' tails have weakened vertebrae.
Another pygmy, the western pygmy, is the smallest venomous snake in the United States.
The venom is hemotoxic
Like pythons and boas, corn snakes are constrictors, which suffocate their prey.
Eastern corn snakes spend much of their time underground prowling through rodent burrows.
Rattlesnakes inject venom into their prey by biting them with curved fangs.
Kingsnakes are known for eating other snakes.
Kingsnakes kill their prey via constriction.
Contrary to popular belief, cottonmouths are capable of biting underwater.
The Florida kingsnake (L. g. floridana) may interbreed with the Eastern kingsnake (L. g. getula).
When disturbed, the Florida pine snake will inflate and rear the front of its body off the ground while hissing very loudly.
The pine snake is unusual because it digs its own burrow for egg laying.
Salt-excreting glands on the tongues of gharials help them tolerate saline (salty) environments.
Of all crocodilian species, gharials have the largest eggs, weighing about 160 g (6.4 oz.) each.
Hatchlings weigh 75 to 130 g (2.6—4.6 oz.) and measure 32.5 to 39.2 cm (12.8—15.4 in.).
Like all reptiles, gharials are ectothermic, depending on external heat sources to regulate their body temperature.
Very agile in aquatic environments, gharials have flattened, muscular tails and webbed feet that help propel them through water.
Gharials frequently bask in the sun to increase their body temperature, resulting in better mobility and digestion.
Gray rat snakes are among the longest snakes in North America, occasionally reaching lengths of 8 feet.
Gray rat snakes can often be found in trees, hiding in crevices or searching for food.
Unlike other rat snakes, gray rat snakes retain their juvenile coloration (speckled pattern) as adults.
Though anacondas are excellent swimmers, they will also hang from branches to dry off.
Iguanas are able to hold their breath for up to 30 minutes.
Newborn green tree pythons are bright yellow, orange, or brick red and do not develop green coloring for 6-8 months.
Milksnakes are known for eating other snakes.
Milksnakes have one of the widest distributions of any snake in the Americas.
Komodo dragons are found on only four islands in Indonesia, with a total area approximately the size of Rhode Island!
After hatching, the young immediately climb trees to avoid being eaten by their cannibalistic elders.
Nearly all geckos have a voice, ranging from a small squeak to the deafening whistles of the African whistling gecko.
Geckos are small lizards with flattened bodies and toe pads that let them run on vertical and overhanging surfaces.
Malaysian water monitors can remain underwater for up to 30 minutes, which comes in handy when hunting aquatic prey.
These water monitors are comfortable in both fresh water and salt water and have been spotted swimming far out in the ocean.
While they are at home in the desert, beaded lizards actually need a higher humidity than other desert reptiles.
When food is scarce, the lizard metabolizes fat stored in its tail.
The rhino iguana is shorter than the more common green iguana but can weigh over twice as much.
Ball pythons are believed to be the longest-living snake species in managed settings, with some individuals reaching their forties.
The scarlet king snake likely derives some protection from potential predators through its mimetic coloration.
Kingsnakes have one of the widest distributions of any American snake.
Sea snakes are front-fanged and highly venomous.
The green sea turtle gets its name from the color of its body fat.
Sea turtles' long, paddlelike flippers are adapted to locomotion in the water. Sea turtles are strong swimmers and divers.
In defense, the skink is able to make a sharp hissing noise and can deliver a savage bite.
These geckoes can inflict severe bites if they are sufficiently threatened.
In parts of Southeast Asia, tokays are regarded as bringers of luck, good fortune, and fertility.
Tomistomas belong to the crocodilian order which also includes alligators, crocodiles, caimans and gharials.
Tomistomas have between 76 and 84 teeth.
The tomistoma name is derived from the Greek words tomos (meaning "sharp") and stoma (meaning "mouth").
Yellow-footed tortoises are named for the yellow-orange scales on their front legs.
Yellow rat snakes spend much time underground prowling through rodent burrows.
In parts of the Trilobite Wilderness, virtually every piece of extracted rock contains trilobite fossils.
There are estimated to be more than 10^31 bacteriophages on the planet.
Fireflies produce a 'cold light', with no infrared or ultraviolet frequencies.
Male moths can track a female releasing pheromones from up to about a kilometer away, using their highly sensitive antennae.
Hyperion, a California coast redwood, is the tallest living tree reliably measured, reaching 116.22 metres (381.3 ft) in 2026.
A quaking aspen grove known as Pando, in the Wasatch Mountains of Utah, is estimated to be around 16,000 years old.
Because most of a saguaro cactus is water, a large plant may weigh 80 pounds for each foot of its main trunk.
Many of the bright colors in Yellowstone's hydrothermal basins come from thermophiles, microorganisms that thrive in hot temperatures.
Kelp forests harbor a greater variety and higher diversity of plants and animals than almost any other ocean community.
In the ocean, pressure increases by one atmosphere for every 33 feet (10.06 meters) of depth.
Rogue waves, which scientists call extreme storm waves, are waves greater than twice the size of the surrounding waves.
Explorers have seen less than 0.001 percent of the deep ocean seafloor, an area roughly the size of Rhode Island.
Pronghorn have true horns, but unlike other horned animals they shed and regrow the horn sheaths every year.
Killer whales are one of the few species known to undergo menopause, along with humans and several other toothed whales.
During the fastest part of their growth, the antlers of a mature bull elk grow about two-thirds of an inch each day.
The maximum reported size for a Pacific bluefin tuna is 3 meters (9.8 feet) long and 450 kilograms (990 pounds).
Dolphins tend to have cone-shaped teeth and prominent beaks, while porpoises have smaller mouths and spade-shaped teeth.
South Florida is the only place where the American alligator and the American crocodile coexist.
Bats are the only true flying mammals.
Almost all of Earth's volcanoes are hidden under the oceans.
The North American plate moves west over the Pacific Ocean basin at roughly the rate at which fingernails grow.
NOAA's official value for the total length of the U.S. shoreline is 95,471 miles (153,646 kilometers).
Badwater Basin in Death Valley is the lowest point in North America, at 282 feet (86 meters) below sea level.
In ideal conditions, kelp can grow up to 18 inches per day.
Giant sequoias, the largest trees in the world, are known to reach ages of up to 3,400 years.
The Joshua tree was once classified as a giant lily, but modern DNA research has placed it in the Agave family.
Great Basin bristlecone pines are the oldest non-clonal species on the planet, surviving for thousands of years.
Yellowstone National Park has more than 500 active geysers, more than half of the world's geysers.
North America's tallest mountain, Mount McKinley in Alaska's Denali National Park, is 20,310 feet tall.
Crater Lake in Oregon is the deepest lake in the United States, at 1,943 feet (592 meters).
Some humpback whale populations swim 5,000 miles between tropical breeding grounds and colder feeding grounds.
Northern elephant seals spend about nine months of the year in the ocean, most of that time underwater.
The giant manta ray is the world's largest ray, with a wingspan of up to 26 feet.
Pronghorn can run sprints at 45 to 50 miles per hour, an adaptation to outrun a cheetah that is now extinct.
The green turtle is named for the color of its fat, not its shell.
The fin whale is the second-largest whale species on Earth, second only to the blue whale.
Gray whales make one of the longest annual migrations of any mammal, traveling about 10,000 miles round-trip.
Evidence suggests that bowhead whales can live for more than 200 years.
The Gulf Stream transports an amount of water greater than that carried by all of the world's rivers combined.
The Greenland shark is the longest-lived known vertebrate; radiocarbon dating of 28 sharks revealed lifespans of at least 272 years.
The highest tides in the world are found in Canada's Bay of Fundy, at Burntcoat Head in Nova Scotia.
The world's largest waterfall lies beneath the Denmark Strait, between Iceland and Greenland, where cold water plunges nearly two miles.
There is only one global ocean, which covers 71 percent of the Earth and is divided into named regions.
Mammoth Cave in Kentucky is the world's longest known cave system.
The highest recorded air temperature, 134°F (57°C), was measured at Furnace Creek in Death Valley on July 10, 1913.
Over deep water, tsunami waves may travel as fast as jet planes, slowing down only when they reach shallow water.
Scientists estimate the ocean produces roughly half of Earth's oxygen, mostly through tiny photosynthetic plankton.
The killer whale, or orca, is the largest member of the dolphin family.
The deepest known part of the ocean, the Challenger Deep in the Mariana Trench, is approximately 10,935 meters (35,876 feet) deep.
